In this series, we are interviewing clinical academics from across King's Health Partners to highlight the different paths you can take towards a clinical academic career. ","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"","transformations":"w=2347&h=440&crop=780,440,171,0","asset":{"entryTitle":"KCATO Banner No Text large","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"f2dfbdf1-0d7d-4fa0-b3a0-4264bfc9fcee","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fkcato\u002Fkcato-banner-no-text-large.x397879c7.jpg?w=2347&h=440&crop=780,440,171,0"}}},"sys":{"id":"9f77199a-29ae-42eb-94b0-c696f7328f3b","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"spotlight","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fspotlight\u002Fspotlight-on-health-professional-researchers-martina-spinoglio"}},"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThis time, we&rsquo;re speaking with Dr Sam Irving. Sam is a clinical academic postdoctoral researcher and clinical scientist. She&rsquo;s also an Associate Director for KCATO, representing professions allied to healthcare including allied health professionals (AHPs), healthcare scientists, pharmacists and psychologists. Her own research focuses on small airway assessment in children with respiratory disease.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"image","value":{"altText":"","transformations":null,"caption":"","asset":{"entryTitle":"sam irving higher res","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"f08d38ac-95bd-40f9-b758-ad5ce87f4dca","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fkcato\u002Fsam-irving-higher-res.x3cfc5b21.jpg"}}}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ECould you give us a brief summary of your route into a PhD, including previous research experience?\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMy research very much grew out of an observation I made in clinical practice. For some of our young patients with rare disease, their clinical lung function results did not seem to match their symptoms. I didn&rsquo;t know why this was, and when I approached our senior leads, they didn&rsquo;t know either, so it became clear the only way to find out was to carry out some research. I&rsquo;d always liked the idea of doing further study, as my undergraduate degree contained a large research module, and so I was delighted when one of our senior professors offered to supervise my PhD to try and find the cause of the results we were seeing.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EFor me, it was very important even at this stage that my research was based on my clinical work. I did the PhD part-time over 6 years, so it was a pretty big commitment, and if this wasn&rsquo;t a topic I felt passionately about clinically I think it would have been much harder.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat are your long-term ambitions for your clinical academic career?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EMy current focus is to gain further fellowship funding for the next stage of my career, establishing myself as an independent researcher. I&rsquo;ve recently been awarded an NIHR Senior Clinical and Practitioner Research Award (SCPRA), and during that time I&rsquo;m going to be putting together the protocol for a large multicentre study looking at lung function in children with bronchiectasis, a rare lung condition. This will take me out of my comfort zone as I&rsquo;ve never led anything this big before, but I&rsquo;m excited and I&rsquo;m up for the challenge!\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EI don&rsquo;t think I&rsquo;ll ever want to give up clinical work entirely though, I really do think my best research ideas are driven by my clinical practice, and my research work makes me a better informed and more effective clinician. I can&rsquo;t imagine now not having both these strands to my work.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EI&rsquo;m also passionate about my role with KCATO. I feel very strongly that clinicians doing research is life changing, both for them and for their patients, and making sure our brilliant people have the support they need to become clinical academics is so important to me.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"I feel very strongly that clinicians doing research is life changing, both for them and for their patients, and making sure our brilliant people have the support they need to become clinical academics is so important to me.","source":null}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat tips would you give to a clinician embarking on an out-of-programme research project, such as a PhD or MD(Res)?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EWe talk a lot about resilience and how necessary it is for a clinical academic career, as there are always going to be disappointments. But I don&rsquo;t think we talk enough about how you develop and build that resilience. It&rsquo;s so important that you have a good, supportive team around you, both your immediate supervisors but also colleagues and mentors that will help give you that sense of community that we need to really thrive. Make sure you maintain some work-life balance - no-one does their best work when they are exhausted and burnt out, and the support of our families and friends is so important to stay well. Many funders will now offer the opportunity to continue with some paid clinical work, and for me staying in touch with my clinical colleagues and my patients is really important to stay focused on why my research work matters.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"We talk a lot about resilience and how necessary it is for a clinical academic career, as there are always going to be disappointments.  But I don’t think we talk enough about how you develop and build that resilience. ","source":null}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat support has been most helpful to you in terms of navigating your clinical academic career to date?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EI can&rsquo;t really overstate how important mentorship has been to me. I&rsquo;m lucky that my original, very inspiring PhD supervisor has become a mentor figure to me, but I&rsquo;ve also benefitted from the skills, expertise and advice of several other very brilliant academics and clinicians as I&rsquo;ve progressed throughout my career.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EI don&rsquo;t think there&rsquo;s any one recipe for mentorship success, my relationships with mine are as different from each other as they are. But they&rsquo;ve all helped me better understand how to best navigate the complexities of a clinical academic career and I&rsquo;m very grateful to all of them.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat is the most rewarding thing about being a health professional researcher?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003ERealising you&rsquo;ve improved care for patients beyond your own clinical sphere of practice. Ultimately, as clinicians there&rsquo;s only so many patients we can see over our careers, regardless of how hard we try. But if you carry out research, design and publish what great care looks like for your patients, then patients you&rsquo;ll never meet in centres you&rsquo;ve never even visited will still benefit from your best practice. And that&rsquo;s so rewarding.&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"If you carry out research, design and publish what great care looks like for your patients, then patients you’ll never meet in centres you’ve never even visited will still benefit from your best practice.","source":null}}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Technology & Science","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"60c84afe-f51e-4e80-af7d-188d9eff995c","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}},{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"entryTitle":"Spotlight on Health Professional Researchers: Dr Sam Irving"},{"summary":"This month, King’s Clinical Academic Training Office (KCATO) is collaborating with the Faculty of Dentistry, Oral & Craniofacial Sciences to spotlight clinical academics working in the field of dentistry. In this edition, we hear from Dr Maisa Seppala, Senior Clinical Lecturer and Honorary Consultant in Orthodontics. development","sys":{"owner":"k2584377","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"spotlight","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fspotlight\u002Fteething-problems-how-disrupted-cell-signalling-affects-tooth-development"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fspotlight\u002Fteething-problems-how-disrupted-cell-signalling-affects-tooth-development","version":{"publishedBy":"k2584377","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2584377","created":"2026-03-04T11:37:51.1707766Z","versionNo":"2.0","modified":"2026-03-04T11:44:59.0295247Z","modifiedBy":"k2584377","published":"2026-03-04T11:45:00.8564602Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"7f93a465-000b-413a-8dbb-ca977a5d56e0","projectId":"website","slug":"teething-problems-how-disrupted-cell-signalling-affects-tooth-development","properties":{}},"relatedCourses":[],"relatedResearch":[],"featuredImage":{"altText":"Headshot of Dr Maisa Seppala","transformations":"w=780&h=1027&crop=780,440,0,137","caption":"","asset":{"entryTitle":"Maisa_Seppala","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"3bef4a63-591d-4658-a658-701262b6c860","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fkcato\u002Fmaisa-seppala.x42be57a2.jpg?w=780&h=1027&crop=780,440,0,137"}}},"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Maisa Seppala","entryDescription":"Senior Clinical Lecturer and Honorary Consultant in Orthodontics","entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"894ee631-cabf-4373-90d9-2bd569ff4ac4","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fmaisa-seppala"}}],"authorByline":null,"relatedSpotlightArticle":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"image","value":{"altText":"","transformations":null,"caption":"","asset":{"entryTitle":"Maisa_Seppala","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"3bef4a63-591d-4658-a658-701262b6c860","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fkcato\u002Fmaisa-seppala.x42be57a2.jpg"}}}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EAdults typically have 32 teeth, but how is their number and form regulated, and why do some people end up with too many? Dr Maisa Seppala is studying the molecular pathways underpinning tooth development, and why this sometimes goes wrong.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMaisa qualified as a dentist in Finland before starting her research journey here at King&rsquo;s. She completed her PhD with Professor Martyn Cobourne, now Academic Head of Orthodontics at King&rsquo;s, after obtaining an EU Early-Stage Marie Curie Research Fellowship to fund her studies. At the time, Professor Cobourne was collaborating with a research laboratory in Baltimore, USA, who had generated mice lacking a gene called \u003Cem\u003EGrowth Arrest-Specific 1 (Gas1)\u003C\u002Fem\u003E. Maisa joined Professor Cobourne&rsquo;s laboratory for her PhD project, which focused on exploring how the craniofacial development of \u003Cem\u003EGas1\u003C\u002Fem\u003E mutant mice differed from that of wild type mice. She found that loss of Gas1 resulted in microform holoprosencephaly (HPE), a condition that causes various facial midline defects\u003Csup\u003E(1)\u003C\u002Fsup\u003E. Significantly, the most severe form of HPE is characterised by a failure of the two brain hemispheres to separate, and it is the leading cause of early miscarriages.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe link between Gas1 and HPE was an important finding but, after specialising as an orthodontist, Maisa was intrigued by some other anomalies also seen in \u003Cem\u003EGas1\u003C\u002Fem\u003E mutants, which included formation of supernumerary teeth\u003Csup\u003E(2) \u003C\u002Fsup\u003Eand fused upper incisors\u003Csup\u003E(3)\u003C\u002Fsup\u003E. Due to the close relationship between dental number anomalies and misaligned bites, she decided to focus on these findings and other similar tooth defects. Maisa explained, &ldquo;My recent studies have focused more specifically on genetic causes of dental anomalies and regulation of tooth number. For example, my PhD student Yiran Wang&rsquo;s study examined how dysregulation of Sonic hedgehog (Shh) signalling results in disrupted tooth patterning and cell cycle regulation leading to fused molars.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EToday, Maisa continues to work on developmental mouse models, but her research is also driven by patients in her clinic who present with abnormalities in tooth number and eruption leading to need for orthodontic treatment. To investigate the underlying causes of these abnormalities, Maisa recruits patients who are interested in participating in research studies and collects samples for DNA sequencing to try and identify genetic variants that may be responsible for these dental anomalies. Current projects in the lab include work on aiming to identify causes of two different conditions; one called &lsquo;primary failure of tooth eruption&rsquo;, where teeth initially erupt but then become stuck to the surrounding bone, and another known as &lsquo;odontoma&rsquo; that is a benign tumour of dental origin and can arise during tooth development, often also preventing the normal eruption of adjacent teeth. Maisa hopes that understanding the molecular mechanisms that cause the formation of these tumours could help healthcare professionals distinguish these benign odontomas from harmful tumours and help choose the best treatment approach where differential diagnosis is needed.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMaisa&rsquo;s interest in research is a longstanding one. She originally considered studying biochemistry at university, but she felt that a career in healthcare was closer to her heart and wanted a profession with more security. &ldquo;I&rsquo;ve learnt that many people who have chosen healthcare or research as their profession have experienced something significant that made a long-lasting impact on us, inspired us to ask questions and made us passionate about merging these experiences to our area of interest in clinical practice or research,&rdquo; Maisa said. &ldquo;My little brother fell ill when I was 5 years old and, throughout his illness, my two elder sisters and I all helped to look after him. It made me question why he fell ill and not me. I obtained my answer from biology lessons during lectures on Mendelian inheritance, which I found fascinating.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAnother key moment came during dental school, when Maisa learnt about Finnish researcher&rsquo;s Irma Thesleff&rsquo;s pioneering work on the molecular basis of tooth development. &ldquo;I remember whispering to my friend next to me that; this is exactly the kind of research I would like to do in the future. I realised it would allow me to combine my interest in genetics while at the same time having a career in dentistry&rdquo;, Maisa said. &ldquo;I did not know at the time that I&rsquo;d be able to fulfil my ambition in a such a fantastic research environment as where I work now, in the Centre for Craniofacial and Regenerative Biology.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMaisa told us that &ldquo;training to become a clinical academic can be long, but full of valuable experiences and opportunities&rdquo;. After her undergraduate studies and PhD, she became a Senior House Officer (SHO) at the maxillofacial surgery department in one of the London&rsquo;s trauma centres. &ldquo;I learned first-hand about the downsides of the London&rsquo;s busy night life, including knife crime and street violence,&rdquo; Maisa said. &ldquo;Our common on-call duties consisted of suturing facial lacerations and diagnosing broken facial bones.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EFollowing SHO, Maisa successfully applied for an NIHR-funded Walport Academic Fellowship position at King&rsquo;s that included orthodontic specialty training. &ldquo;For my clinical part of the training, I rotated between St George&rsquo;s and King&rsquo;s College Hospital while continuing research at King&rsquo;s,&rdquo; Maisa explained. &ldquo;I then began post-CCST (Certificate of Completion of Specialty Training) training, treating more complex patients and completing exams on hospital management and critical appraisal. I also became a Lecturer in Orthodontics at King&rsquo;s and, as part of this new position, I needed to complete a Fellowship of the Higher Education Academy (FHEA) degree, learning more theory related to teaching.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIn her current position as Honorary Consultant (Guy&rsquo;s and St Thomas&rsquo; Hospital) and Senior Clinical Lecturer in Orthodontics (King&rsquo;s College London), Maisa divides her time between treating patients, teaching and supervising specialty trainees in the orthodontic clinic, pursuing her own research, supervising research students, and leading one of the modules that is part of the BSc in Dental Therapy and Hygiene programme. She has also served in various university roles as a member in the Curriculum &amp; Assessment, the International, the Working Allocation Model (WAM) and the Athena Swan committees as well as acted as the Departmental Audit lead. As part of the research commitments, she has also had the opportunity to collaborate internationally and present at various conferences, which is something she particularly enjoys about her role.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E&ldquo;I must say, although my training has been long and at times challenging, and although I had to balance things with starting a family of three boys at the same time, it has given me a very versatile and rewarding role,&rdquo; Maisa said. &ldquo;Dentists pursuing their undergraduate degrees might not realise how many interesting opportunities for postgraduate training and research there exists, but I hope my story demonstrates some of the different paths one can choose to do as part of their dental career.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EReferences:\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E(1)\u003Cu\u003E Seppala M\u003C\u002Fu\u003E, Depew MJ, Martinelli DC, Fan CM, Sharpe PT, Cobourne MT. &rdquo;Gas1 is a modifier for holoprosencephaly and genetically interacts with Sonic hedgehog&rdquo;. Journal of Clinical Investigation. (2007) Jun;117(6):1575-84. doi: 10.1172\u002FJCI32032\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E(2) \u003Cu\u003ESeppala M\u003C\u002Fu\u003E, Thivichon-Prince B, Xavier GM, Shaffie N, Sangani I, Birjandi AA, Rooney J, Lau JNS, Dhaliwal R, Rossi O, Riaz MA, Stonehouse-Smith D, Wang Y, Papegeorgiou SN, Viriot L, Cobourne MT. &ldquo;Gas1 regulates patterning of the murine and human dentitions through Shh&rdquo;. Journal of Dental Research. (2022 Apr;101(4):473-482. doi: 10.1177\u002F00220345211049403\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E(3)\u003Cu\u003E Seppala M\u003C\u002Fu\u003E, Xavier GM, Fan CM, Cobourne MT. &ldquo;Boc modifies the spectrum of holoprosencephaly in the absence of Gas1 function&rdquo; Biol Open. (2014) Jul 25;3(8):728-40. doi: 10.1242\u002Fbio.20147989\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Technology & Science","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"60c84afe-f51e-4e80-af7d-188d9eff995c","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}},{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"entryTitle":"Teething problems: how disrupted cell signalling affects tooth development"},{"summary":"King's Clinical Academic Training Office (KCATO) offers guidance, funding opportunities, professional development programmes and events for research-active health professionals. on Health Professional Researchers: Sean Harrington","entryDescription":"King's Clinical Academic Training Office (KCATO) offers guidance, funding opportunities, professional development programmes and events for research-active health professionals. In this series, we are interviewing clinical academics from across King's Health Partners to highlight the different paths you can take towards a clinical academic career. ","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"Headshot of Sean Harrington","caption":"","transformations":"w=780&h=1040&crop=780,440,0,312","asset":{"entryTitle":"Sean Harrington photo","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"37a9d79d-353c-4e84-911b-9e39b4c02789","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fkcato\u002Fsean-harrington-photo.x6a50bf9d.jpeg?w=780&h=1040&crop=780,440,0,312"}}},"sys":{"id":"12ad268a-25d4-44d4-86ed-d25bc8dbdc30","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"spotlight","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fspotlight\u002Fspotlight-on-health-professional-researchers-sean-harrington"}},"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EMartina Spinoglio started her NIHR INSIGHT Research Masters Studentship in October 2024. These studentships provide a stipend and cover tuition fees to study a research master&rsquo;s course at King&rsquo;s or one of our partnering universities. The studentships are aimed at nurses, midwives and professions allied to healthcare. Martina began her career as a midwife before moving into sonography, using soundwaves to assess fetal cardiac health. In this interview, she discusses how her studentship supported this career transition and has equipped her with the tools to combine clinical work with research going forwards.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003ECan you tell us about your current role, and your career so far? \u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EI am currently working as a fetal cardiac sonographer. I initially qualified as a midwife in Rome in 2015 and moved to London in 2016, where I began working as a midwife.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAlongside my clinical work, I developed a growing interest in fetal ultrasound, which led me to undertake the Medical Ultrasound programme at King&rsquo;s College London in 2019. I completed a Postgraduate Certificate in Obstetric and Gynaecological Ultrasound in 2020 and subsequently moved into obstetric ultrasound and fetal medicine as an obstetric sonographer.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAs my experience developed, I became particularly interested in fetal anatomy and, more specifically, fetal cardiology. As part of my Postgraduate Diploma, I undertook a specialist placement in fetal cardiac ultrasound and graduated in 2024. This qualification enabled me to start working as a fetal cardiac sonographer, where I continue to build my expertise.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhy did you decide to apply for the INSIGHT Programme?&nbsp;\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003ESince beginning my Medical Ultrasound studies in 2019, I had a strong aspiration to complete the full MSc programme, including the research project. When the opportunity arose to propose a module of choice for the Postgraduate Diploma, I was able to design a fetal cardiac ultrasound module and re-enter the programme. This decision was academically fulfilling but also financially challenging, as I was self-funded throughout my studies.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDuring this period, my supervisor introduced me to the NIHR INSIGHT Programme as a potential route to continue my academic development. It represented an accessible and realistic opportunity to explore research alongside my clinical career and to progress towards a role that would combine both clinical and academic work.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat did you study during your time on the INSIGHT Programme?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EDuring my time on the NIHR INSIGHT Programme, I completed an MSc in Clinical Ultrasound at King&rsquo;s College London, which involved a research project focused on fetal medicine and fetal cardiology. My research investigated the feasibility and effectiveness of a first trimester screening for congenital heart disease, performed at the time of the routine screening for common aneuploidies (Down&rsquo;s syndrome, Edward&rsquo;s syndrome and Patau&rsquo;s syndrome &ndash; most common chromosomal abnormalities) between 11 and 14 weeks of gestation. The aim of my research was to identify significant differences in characteristics, outcomes, and ultrasound image properties between cases of congenital heart defect detected during the first trimester of pregnancy and those diagnosed later in pregnancy.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"My research investigated the feasibility and effectiveness of a first trimester screening for congenital heart disease.","source":null}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EHow have you balanced your research master&rsquo;s course with your professional role in health and care? Do you have any advice gained from this experience that would be useful for future applicants?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EBalancing my MSc with my clinical role was definitely challenging. Working clinically meant that my days were already demanding, particularly in fetal cardiology where rare cardiac pathology is frequently encountered and a high level of specialist knowledge and independent study is required. Adding a research degree alongside this required careful time management, discipline, and a lot of personal commitment. What made it sustainable and motivating was that my research topic was closely aligned with my clinical practice and deeply important to me. My MSc focused on first trimester detection of congenital heart defects, and my daily clinical work continually reinforced the relevance of this research and its potential to improve patient care.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIn terms of advice for future applicants, I would say: First, choose a topic you are genuinely passionate about. When your research matters to you personally and professionally, it doesn&rsquo;t feel like an extra burden, it feels purposeful. Second, be realistic and organised. A research degree alongside clinical work requires planning and honest conversations with your team and supervisors about workload and expectations. Third, don&rsquo;t underestimate the value of clinical insight. As health professionals, we are in a unique position to identify gaps in practice and ask meaningful questions that directly improve patient care.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"Choose a topic you are genuinely passionate about. When your research matters to you personally and professionally, it doesn’t feel like an extra burden, it feels purposeful.","source":null}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EHow has the INSIGHT Programme influenced your career path?&nbsp;\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EThe NIHR INSIGHT Programme strengthened my interest in research alongside clinical practice. Through this experience, I developed a greater interest and a better understanding of the research process. It also supported my transition into a fetal cardiac sonographer role. The programme has encouraged me to consider future involvement in research activities alongside my clinical work.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat will be your next step after completing your NIHR INSIGHT research master&rsquo;s, and how do you plan to use research as part of your future career?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EMy immediate next step is to continue integrating research within my clinical practice in fetal medicine and fetal cardiology. I am particularly interested in advancing early detection pathways for congenital heart defects, especially building on my MSc work around first trimester cardiac assessment. There is still significant variation in early detection rates in England, and I would like to contribute to research that improves consistency, training, and implementation across services.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ELonger term, I hope to develop as a clinical academic sonographer, maintaining a strong clinical role while leading or collaborating on research projects that directly influence scanning protocols, education, and patient pathways. I am interested in pursuing further research focused on improving early cardiac screening and exploring how we can optimise sonographer training with the ultimate goal of improving early detection rates of major congenital heart defects.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"I am interested in pursuing further research focused on improving early cardiac screening and exploring how we can optimise sonographer training with the ultimate goal of improving early detection rates of major congenital heart defects.","source":null}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat is the most rewarding thing about being a research-active health professional?\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EThe most rewarding aspect of being a research-active health professional is seeing how research directly improves patient care. In my role as a fetal cardiac sonographer, research allows me to evaluate practice, identify knowledge gaps, and contribute to improvements that benefit families.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIt also supports continual learning, professional growth, and collaboration with multidisciplinary teams. The greatest satisfaction for me comes from knowing that research can lead to earlier detection of conditions, better counselling, and improved outcomes, which was the focus of my research.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EIs there any support that have you found particularly helpful while navigating your clinical academic career?&nbsp;\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EOne of the most helpful forms of support in developing my clinical academic career has been the feedback from both my academic and clinical supervisors. Having guidance from both perspectives was essential in supporting my growth. Balancing their expectations was sometimes challenging, particularly when managing differing priorities, but ongoing communication and constructive feedback helped align these demands. Ultimately, the combination of academic and clinical supervision strengthened the relevance of my research to clinical practice and increased my confidence in managing a dual career pathway.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EWhat advice would you give to a health professional who&rsquo;s looking to get into research?&nbsp;\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EI would encourage health professionals to consider research as a highly rewarding career, particularly when combined with clinical practice. Maintaining a clinical role allows you to see the real-world impact of research through improved patient care, which can be highly motivating. My biggest piece of advice would be to choose a research topic you are genuinely passionate about, as this makes the challenges of research, such as setbacks, time pressures, and uncertainty, much easier to manage.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EI would also recommend seeking early mentorship and being open to exploring different research opportunities to find an area that truly aligns with your interests and values.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EFinally, what do you enjoy doing in your spare time?&nbsp;\u003Cbr\u003E\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003EIn my spare time, I enjoy a range of activities, but I particularly enjoy travelling. Exploring new places and cultures helps me relax and gain new perspectives. Travelling also gives me the opportunity to step away from routine and return with renewed motivation and energy.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Technology & Science","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"60c84afe-f51e-4e80-af7d-188d9eff995c","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}},{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"entryTitle":"Spotlight 780×440","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"a4f00c7f-2bff-4c64-9c0a-861566588839","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Ffolsm\u002Fthumbnail-pages\u002Fslcps\u002Fstroke-research-group-thumbnail-780×440.x80cbb57d.png"}}},"sys":{"id":"6e3b6252-fadd-4a7d-a698-1c06dff12d55","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"researchProfile","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fresearch\u002Fstroke-research-group"}}],"publishDate":"2026-05-06T15:00:00","searchOptions":{"includeInKCLSiteSearch":true,"includeInSitemap":true,"includeInKCLSiteListings":true},"entryDescription":"New research presented at the European Stroke Organisation Conference (ESOC) 2026 shows that after decades of decline, stroke incidence is rising again, driven by higher rates in some ethnic minority and socioeconomically disadvantaged groups.","relatedEvent":null,"title":"Research presented at ESOC 2026 suggests rising stroke rates due to widening ethnic and socioeconomic inequalities","sys":{"owner":"k2477673","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fresearch-presented-at-esoc-2026-suggests-rising-stroke-rates-due-to-widening-ethnic-and-socioeconomic-inequalities"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fresearch-presented-at-esoc-2026-suggests-rising-stroke-rates-due-to-widening-ethnic-and-socioeconomic-inequalities","version":{"publishedBy":"k2477673","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2477673","created":"2026-05-06T11:19:44.7339754Z","versionNo":"1.0","modified":"2026-05-06T14:15:29.9432305Z","modifiedBy":"k2477673","published":"2026-05-06T14:15:42.0256485Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"5b9e4662-4df4-48da-86e6-36249eb9e169","projectId":"website","slug":"research-presented-at-esoc-2026-suggests-rising-stroke-rates-due-to-widening-ethnic-and-socioeconomic-inequalities","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[],"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Iain Marshall","entryDescription":"Clinical Reader in Population Health Science","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"","transformations":"w=203&h=203&crop=160,160,19,17","asset":{"entryTitle":"Iain Marshall","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"d87b85b2-9d36-4fde-8773-1260e5aaf874","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fperson-profile\u002F2022b\u002Fiain-marshall1.x89dca1b5.png?w=203&h=203&crop=160,160,19,17"}}},"sys":{"id":"aa131b5e-f17b-4237-a768-78b8360a498e","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fiain-marshall"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe findings reflect broader patterns seen in diverse urban populations internationally, pointing to widening health inequalities and highlighting an urgent need to improve uptake of cardiovascular risk programmes across all socioeconomic and ethnic groups.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThese findings come from the South London Stroke Register (SLSR), one of the longest-running population-based stroke registers in the world. The SLSR is led by Dr Iain Marshall in the Stroke Research Group at King's College London.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe SLSR recruits every person with a first-ever stroke within a geographically defined, ethnically diverse area of South London, and investigates how stroke affects communities over time, including people who may never reach specialist care.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe 30-year analysis, presented at ESOC 2026 by lead researcher Dr Camila Pantoja-Ruiz from King&rsquo;s, examined how the risk of having stroke varied in different ethnic and socioeconomic groups in a population of 333,000 people in South London, of whom 7,726 had a stroke. After a 34% decrease between 1995&ndash;1999 and 2010&ndash;2014 (from 198 to 131 cases per 100,000 people), the risk of stroke increased by 13% in 2020&ndash;2024.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAlthough overall trends initially improved, the recent rise was not evenly distributed. In 2020&ndash;2024, stroke incidence was more than twice as high in Black African and Black Caribbean populations compared with the White population.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ERates remained consistently higher in these groups across the study period, with the highest incidence observed among those experiencing socioeconomic deprivation. These patterns are consistent with evidence from other high-income settings.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"This trend may partly reflect the lasting impact of the COVID-19 pandemic, which reduced access to primary care, blood pressure monitoring and prescribing, particularly affecting Black and deprived communities.","source":"Dr Camila Pantoja-Ruiz, PhD candidate in the Stroke Research Group at King’s "}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003ECompared with White participants, Black African and Black Caribbean populations were more likely to have high blood pressure (47% and 29% higher prevalence, respectively) and diabetes (92% and 123% higher, respectively), both of which increase stroke risk. Notably, 12% of Black African patients had no diagnosed risk factors prior to stroke, compared with 6.3% of White patients, suggesting gaps in early detection.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E&ldquo;These patterns of increased stroke risk in these communities may also be influenced by broader factors, including racism, unconscious bias and socioeconomic circumstances, which can impact access to and quality of care,&rdquo; added Dr Pantoja-Ruiz.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EEthnic inequalities were greatest for intracerebral haemorrhage, a severe and often fatal subtype of stroke, with disparities between Black African, Black Caribbean and White populations being more pronounced than for other stroke subtypes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDr Pantoja-Ruiz explained that this may be due to differences in underlying risk factors: &ldquo;Compared with other stroke types, intracerebral haemorrhage is more strongly associated with uncontrolled high blood pressure, which is more common in Black communities.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDr Pantoja-Ruiz stated that these inequalities persisted even after taking into account clinical severity, socioeconomic status and other clinical factors. Additional analyses found that Black stroke survivors, particularly Black African survivors, were less likely to receive timely follow-up care, with Black African survivors having 34% lower odds of follow-up.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E&ldquo;The period immediately after a stroke is critical for preventing another,&rdquo; added Dr Pantoja-Ruiz. &ldquo;Interventions such as controlling blood pressure, optimising medication and identifying other vascular risks are essential. Less timely follow-up leaves patients at elevated risk for longer and may be influenced by mistrust in healthcare services linked to historical and ongoing experiences of discrimination.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAdditionally, Black African populations were found to experience stroke around 10&ndash;12 years earlier than White populations on average. These findings highlight the need for earlier prevention and targeted interventions, the researchers say.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"While the specific mechanisms differ between healthcare systems, a consistent finding is that prevention is not reaching those most at risk. Worsening cardiovascular inequalities have been reported across many settings, and these findings add to growing evidence that this is a widespread problem requiring targeted solutions.","source":"Dr Pantoja-Ruiz"}},{"type":"markup","value":null}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"conversationNews":{"storyUrl":null},"doNot":true,"entryTitle":"Research presented at ESOC 2026 suggests rising stroke rates due to widening ethnic and socioeconomic inequalities"},{"entryThumbnail":{"altText":"Traffic jam in the development","sys":{"owner":"k2480256","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fair-pollution-exposure-in-the-womb-linked-to-worse-language-and-motor-development"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fair-pollution-exposure-in-the-womb-linked-to-worse-language-and-motor-development","version":{"publishedBy":"k2480256","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2480256","created":"2026-04-28T16:07:16.901356Z","versionNo":"2.0","modified":"2026-04-29T07:50:28.4455298Z","modifiedBy":"k2480256","published":"2026-04-29T07:50:30.9794373Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"86d4ad7e-9433-4ff5-b819-1ac36083f075","projectId":"website","slug":"air-pollution-exposure-in-the-womb-linked-to-worse-language-and-motor-development","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[{"entryTitle":"Life Course & Population Health Sciences","entryDescription":"Life Course & Population Health Sciences MPhil\u002FPhD\u002F MDRes","entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"da3cc7d8-a951-46e3-a22c-c62c9f2e14b1","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"postgraduateResearchDegree","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fstudy\u002Fpostgraduate-research\u002Fareas\u002Fbiomedical-engineering-imaging-sciences-mphil-phd"}}],"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Professor Serena Counsell","entryDescription":"Professor of Perinatal Imaging & Health","entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"cdd7308d-5955-441a-8fc4-6250a4e6b08f","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fserena-counsell"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe King&rsquo;s College London study is the first to investigate pollution exposure and development in Greater London, measuring language and motor skills of infants whose mothers were pregnant in the capital.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EPollution exposure during the first trimester of pregnancy (conception to week 12\u002F13), emitted from sources such as traffic, was linked to worse language skills at 18 months.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe research is also the first to address whether being born prematurely changes the impact of pollution exposure during pregnancy. The study found that pollution had an even greater impact on babies born pre-term, with this group showing poorer motor as well as language skills.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe research follows the same group of babies found to have different brain structure sizes, attributed pollution exposure in the womb. Lead author Dr Alexandra Bonthrone, who completed the research at School of Biomedical Engineering &amp; Imaging Sciences, at King&rsquo;s College London, said: &ldquo;We wanted to establish if air pollution levels related to cognitive, language and motor abilities in this critical period of early childhood. The first 1,000 days from conception to age two shape long-term brain health and behaviour.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"image","value":{"altText":"Two brain scans - left black and white - right coloured segments in the brain","transformations":null,"caption":"High resolution neonatal brain MRI from a typical infant in the Developing Human Connectome Project. The brain MRI processed to ‘segment’ out different types of intracranial tissue.","asset":{"entryTitle":"High resolution neonatal brain MRI from a typical infant in the Developing Human Connectome Project","entryDescription":"High resolution neonatal brain MRI from a typical infant in the Developing Human Connectome Project. The brain MRI processed to ‘segment’ out different types of intracranial tissue.","sys":{"id":"3c83f7f8-cb71-41c6-8016-18fe630f5219","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fhigh-resolution-neonatal-brain-mri-from-a-typical-infant-in-the-developing-human-connectome-project.x67a92bc4.png"}}}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003E498 infants from Greater London were involved in the study, recruited from St Thomas&rsquo; Hospital between 2015-2020. Of the cohort, 125 were born prematurely, and within that group 54 born less than 32 weeks &ndash; known as &lsquo;very and extremely preterm&rsquo;. The infants underwent a standard clinical test of development, known as Bayley Scales, which measures cognitive, language and motor skills &ndash; with scores of 100 reflecting population average.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThose exposed to high pollution in the first trimester of pregnancy scored on average 5-7 points lower compared to babies exposed to low pollution on language tests. There was no association with pollution in second and third trimesters.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EPremature babies exposed to the highest pollution levels in the womb across all of pregnancy, scored on average 11 points less than those exposed to low levels for motor skills.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"While preterm babies are already more likely to have difficulties with motor development, we adjusted for different clinical factors, like time spent on support for breathing, to confirm the relationship between air pollution and motor development.","source":"Dr Bonthrone, School of Biomedical Engineering & Imaging Sciences, at King’s College London"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe King&rsquo;s team collaborated with the Environmental Research Group at Imperial College London to measure pollutants. The London Air Pollution Toolkit models pollution levels based on amount of traffic and average speeds travelled. This enabled researchers to estimate the amount of pollution mothers were exposed to during pregnancy based on their home postcode.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDr Bonthrone said: &ldquo;The levels in this study of toddlers born in greater London from 2015 to 2020 were within the annual limits set out by the Government in the 2010 legislation on air quality, although they are higher than the safe levels set out by WHO in 2021.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EPollutants analysed included particulate matter and nitrogen dioxide, which are emitted by traffic exhausts. Tiny particles of these molecules can be breathed in by humans. Breathing in air pollution is the second largest risk factor for death in children under 5 globally, second only to malnutrition, according to the State of Global Air Report.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"image","value":{"altText":"Map of Greater London showing higher areas of pollution in the centre, less in areas further away from the centre","transformations":null,"caption":"Annual mean NO2 (ug\u002Fm3) concentration for 2022 in Greater London. Note: the legal limit value for NO2 in the UK is 40 ug\u002Fm3 , while in contrast, the WHO recommends an annual guideline value of 10 ug\u002Fm3 . Credit Modelling Team, Environmental Research Group at Imperial College London","asset":{"entryTitle":"Pollution map 2, credit Modelling Team, ERG","entryDescription":"Annual mean NO2 (ug\u002Fm3) concentration for 2022 in Greater London. Note: the legal limit value for NO2 in the UK is 40 ug\u002Fm3 , while in contrast, the WHO recommends an annual guideline value of 10 ug\u002Fm3 . Credit Modelling Team, Environmental Research Group at Imperial College London","sys":{"id":"be4a6978-74bd-464d-ace7-a5cd4f9ee029","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fpollution-map-2-credit-modelling-team-erg.x862148b4.jpg"}}}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EDr Bonthrone added: &ldquo;At this stage, it is too early to say whether these babies will catch up with their peers &ndash; the only way will be to study them later in childhood. It could be that the development differences have effects into education and information processing, but we won&rsquo;t know for sure until we do future studies.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ESenior-author Professor Serena Counsell, School of Biomedical Engineering &amp; Imaging Sciences, King&rsquo;s College London, added: &ldquo;This works adds to a growing body of evidence that maternal exposure to high levels of air traffic pollution may be associated with altered outcomes in their offspring. Reducing maternal exposure to air traffic pollution should be a public health priority.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"This study shows that even within what we currently call ‘legal’ levels of air pollution, we are seeing measurable impacts on the developing brain. That should fundamentally change how we think about ‘acceptable’ air quality for pregnant women and young children. Improving air quality is not just about cleaner skies; it is about giving every child the best possible start in life.","source":"Professor Frank Kelly, from the Environmental Research Group at Imperial College London, who is also Deputy Director for MRC Centre for Environment and Health"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe latest study, published in the Journal of Physiology, forms part of wider research following a cohort of children who underwent brain MRIs before or soon after birth, known as the \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.developingconnectome.org\u002F\"\u003EDeveloping Human Connectome Project\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis study was supported by the UKRI Medical Research Council (MRC) UK, the European Research Council under the European Union&rsquo;s Seventh Framework Program, the Wellcome\u002FEPSRC Centre for Medical Engineering and a King&rsquo;s Together Award.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDr Bonthrone completed this research while at King&rsquo;s College London. She has recently joined the Centre for Reproductive Health, Institute for Regeneration and Repair, University of Edinburgh.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}},{"entryTitle":"Technology research from the Institute of Psychiatry, Psychology & Neuroscience (IoPPN) at King’s College London has found that experiencing adversity in childhood, adulthood or both is associated with greater frailty in middle-aged and older adults.","relatedEvent":null,"title":"Experiencing adversity linked to greater frailty in middle-aged and older adults","sys":{"owner":"k2143182","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fexperiencing-adversity-linked-to-greater-frailty-in-middle-aged-and-older-adults"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fexperiencing-adversity-linked-to-greater-frailty-in-middle-aged-and-older-adults","version":{"publishedBy":"k2031537","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2143182","created":"2026-04-27T14:23:33.2876206Z","versionNo":"2.0","modified":"2026-04-28T15:47:49.6154644Z","modifiedBy":"k2031537","published":"2026-04-28T15:47:51.4766388Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"8446f12a-e147-4981-8318-c49f1fe0319b","projectId":"website","slug":"experiencing-adversity-linked-to-greater-frailty-in-middle-aged-and-older-adults","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[],"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Julian Mutz","entryDescription":"King’s Prize Research Fellow","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"","transformations":"w=160&h=160","asset":{"entryTitle":"Mutz_Julian-1331 web","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"3638c6e2-6e23-4108-a3ff-9ca19633cb33","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fperson-profile\u002F2022b\u002Fmutz-julian-1331-web.x4724d2af.jpg?w=160&h=160"}}},"sys":{"id":"89b8933e-5004-45e4-b2d3-d5effa546443","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fjulian-mutz"}},{"entryTitle":"Monica Aas","entryDescription":"MRC Research Fellow","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"Monica Aas","caption":"","transformations":"w=240&h=160&crop=160,160,40,0","asset":{"entryTitle":"Monica Aas","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"1ca24cc3-4df1-4305-acec-658e5b4dc2a4","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fperson-profile\u002F2022b\u002Fmonica-aas.xeb353126.jpg?w=240&h=160&crop=160,160,40,0"}}},"sys":{"id":"1687bff7-b78c-48c8-9849-15aae65d333d","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fmonica-aas"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EPublished in BMC Medicine, the study analysed data from over 150,000 participants and found the strongest associations in people who experienced adversity in both childhood and adulthood. These individuals were more frail than those exposed during only one life stage or not at all, and they also showed a biological age\u003Csup\u003E1\u003C\u002Fsup\u003E older than their chronological age\u003Csup\u003E2\u003C\u002Fsup\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EFrailty is a clinical syndrome marked by an overall decline in physical resilience and increased vulnerability to adverse health outcomes. As populations age globally, it is becoming an increasingly important public health challenge and a target for prevention and management.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe researchers investigated how adverse events experienced in childhood and adulthood were associated with molecular, clinical and functional markers of biological ageing, including telomere length, metabolomic ageing, frailty and grip strength.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E153,557 participants provided health and sociodemographic data, underwent physical examination and provided biological samples as part of the UK Biobank study. They also completed questionnaires asking about their exposure to adversity and trauma.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EA total of 40,086 participants (27 per cent) reported experiencing adverse events in both their childhood and adulthood. Overall, 63,066 participants (41 per cent) reported at least one adverse event in childhood, while 80,895 (53 per cent) reported at least one adverse event in adulthood.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAn analysis of the data found that adversity at any life stage was associated with higher frailty index scores, with the strongest association seen in those who had experienced adversity in both childhood and adulthood. This group also had a biological age that was older than their chronological age and weaker grip strength.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"“Our findings suggest that adversity across the life course may leave a lasting imprint on health, with the greatest burden seen in individuals exposed in both childhood and adulthood”","source":"Dr Julian Mutz, King’s Prize Research Fellow and the study’s last author"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EDr Julian Mutz, King&rsquo;s Prize Research Fellow and the study&rsquo;s last author, said, &ldquo;While the human body becomes less physically strong as it ages, frailty is not simply about getting older. It reflects the accumulation of health deficits and a marked decline in physiological resilience.\"\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDr Monica Aas, MRC Research Fellow at King&rsquo;s IoPPN and the study&rsquo;s first author, said, &ldquo;While these findings should be taken seriously, there is also reason for optimism. Regular light exercise, good nutrition and strong social connections have all been shown to be effective interventions for frailty, providing clinicians and policy makers with a clear means of prevention and management as the global populace continues to age.&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe researchers now hope to investigate what could mitigate the impact of adversity on biological ageing, including the factors linked to healthy ageing in individuals exposed to adversity or trauma.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThis work was conducted using data from UK Biobank, with funding support provided by the Medical Research Council and the King&rsquo;s Prize Fellowship.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E&nbsp;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cem\u003EAdverse events in both childhood and adulthood are associated with molecular, clinical and functional markers of ageing \u003C\u002Fem\u003E(Aas, Mutz et al) (DOI 10.1186\u002Fs12916-026-04815-x) was published in BMC Medicine.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EFor more information, please contact Patrick O'Brien (\u003Ca href=\"mailto:ioppn-pr@kcl.ac.uk\"\u003EMedia Manager\u003C\u002Fa\u003E)\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"conversationNews":{"storyUrl":null},"doNot":false,"entryTitle":"Experiencing groups","sys":{"owner":"k2030448","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fhousing-reform-unites-voters-but-fails-to-mobilise-disadvantaged-groups"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fhousing-reform-unites-voters-but-fails-to-mobilise-disadvantaged-groups","version":{"publishedBy":"k2480256","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2030448","created":"2026-05-07T08:10:17.6536018Z","versionNo":"2.0","modified":"2026-05-07T08:50:07.4680322Z","modifiedBy":"k2480256","published":"2026-05-07T08:50:12.691077Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"afe07bcb-0b9b-4927-b66e-1f9bf7b7d99b","projectId":"website","slug":"housing-reform-unites-voters-but-fails-to-mobilise-disadvantaged-groups","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[{"entryTitle":"Political Pahontu","entryDescription":"Lecturer in Political Behaviour","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"Raluca L. Pahontu","transformations":"w=160&h=240&crop=160,160,0,17","asset":{"entryTitle":"RalucaPahontu","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"022db4be-a393-47cc-a121-40547a164786","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fperson-profile\u002F2022b\u002Fralucapahontu.xeaf60e30.jpg?w=160&h=240&crop=160,160,0,17"}}},"sys":{"id":"774a6146-6e11-4295-b7e5-83c2db2502bc","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fraluca-pahontu"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe study, by Dr Raluca Pahontu (King&rsquo;s) and Dr Tom O&rsquo;Grady (University College London), examined whether policies designed to reduce housing insecurity could encourage greater electoral engagement among those most affected.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThose on lower incomes and exposed to housing insecurity are typically less likely to vote, contributing to an out-sized political influence for those with higher incomes and in secure housing.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EUsing a survey experiment of 1,790 people, the academics asked respondents about a range of proposed housing measures, including caps on rent increases, bans on evictions, and limits on mortgage interest rates. Participants were asked to choose between hypothetical political candidates offering different combinations of these policies and indicate how likely they would be to vote.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe findings suggest that while such policies are popular, they do not disproportionately motivate those experiencing housing insecurity to vote or engage in the political process. Instead, support was broadly spread across groups, including both the affluent and less secure.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EPolicies aimed at the insecure, such as limiting rent rises, strengthening eviction protections and introducing mandatory quality inspections, were particularly well received. However, the popularity of the policies did not translate into a higher stated turnout among disadvantaged respondents compared with others.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe researchers say housing is a &ldquo;high valence, low priority&rdquo; issue: one where there is broad agreement on what is required to address problems, but which ranks below concerns such as the economy, healthcare and immigration for most voters.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe study concludes that unequal political participation is unlikely to be solved by introducing targeted housing reforms. Instead, lower turnout among disadvantaged groups appears to stem from more deep-rooted factors, such as long-term voting habits and levels of political engagement, rather than a lack of policies addressing their needs.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"postitNote","value":{"alignment":"Full width","title":"Read more...","content":"\u003Cp\u003EYou can read the study in full \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fosf.io\u002Fsb7rh\u002Ffiles\u002Fwxqst\"\u003Ehere\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Society","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"3376166e-dba4-4824-9278-b4b710cddf81","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"conversationNews":{"storyUrl":null},"doNot":false,"entryTitle":"Housing funding award from the European Union.","relatedEvent":null,"title":"€4.8m project will harness AI to tackle children's diarrhoeal diseases in sub-Saharan Africa","sys":{"owner":"k2477673","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002F4.8m-project-will-harness-ai-to-tackle-childrens-diarrhoeal-diseases-in-sub-saharan-africa"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002F4.8m-project-will-harness-ai-to-tackle-childrens-diarrhoeal-diseases-in-sub-saharan-africa","version":{"publishedBy":"k2480256","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2477673","created":"2026-04-28T15:19:09.5993644Z","versionNo":"2.0","modified":"2026-04-29T15:28:14.4682956Z","modifiedBy":"k2480256","published":"2026-04-29T15:28:18.005955Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"5e0b9a70-3acd-46b0-8b89-507697e05e08","projectId":"website","slug":"4.8m-project-will-harness-ai-to-tackle-childrens-diarrhoeal-diseases-in-sub-saharan-africa","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[],"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Tania Dottorini","entryDescription":"Professor of Artificial Intelligence for Science","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"","transformations":"w=253&h=305&crop=160,160,60,75","asset":{"entryTitle":"Tania Dottorini","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"d75f2a33-6d5f-4867-90cc-fe6d4868667e","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fperson-profile\u002F2022b\u002Ftania-dottorini.xbd99af57.png?w=253&h=305&crop=160,160,60,75"}}},"sys":{"id":"afbcf798-8ef6-4fed-92bc-08ec9595c959","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Ftania-dottorini"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe CARE-AFRICA project aims to generate an AI tool that can predict the most likely pathogen responsible for a child&rsquo;s diarrhoeal disease and provide guidance on the most effective treatment options based on that prediction.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ESupported by the \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.global-health-edctp3.europa.eu\u002Findex_en\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"\u003EGlobal Health EDCTP3\u003C\u002Fa\u003E and its members, the project brings together six partners from the UK, Italy, Spain, South Africa, Uganda and Ethiopia, combining expertise in AI, data science, infectious diseases, public health and health information systems.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"Ultimately, we want to be able to equip a frontline healthcare worker with a digital tool with the power to predict the most likely pathogen causing a child’s diarrhoeal infection based on their symptoms. Is it a bacterium or a virus? If it’s a bacterium, what antibiotics are known to be effective against it, and which ones should be avoided?","source":"Tania Dottorini, Professor of AI in Science at King’s and project lead"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe tool will be designed to run on a tablet device, with results generated in five minutes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Ch2\u003EAiming to addressing major global health challenges\u003C\u002Fh2\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDiarrhoea is a leading cause of mortality in children worldwide, with some of the highest death rates in children under five years of age seen in sub-Saharan Africa.\u003Csup\u003E1\u003C\u002Fsup\u003E Diarrhoeal diseases can be challenging to manage &ndash; they are caused by a variety of bacteria, viruses and parasites, making it difficult to identify the pathogen responsible and deliver the most appropriate treatment.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ETo develop the AI tool, the team will train an AI model on a variety of data. These will include clinical data from children in Ethiopia and Uganda infected with pathogens that cause diarrhoea, including Shigella, \u003Cem\u003EVibrio cholerae\u003C\u002Fem\u003E, \u003Cem\u003EEscherichia coli \u003C\u002Fem\u003Eand norovirus. The tool will also integrate environmental, climate, socioeconomic and demographic data linked to infections with these pathogens.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe idea is that when a patient&rsquo;s clinical data is inputted about a current infection, the tool will be able to provide probabilities for the most likely pathogens causing the infection, as well as information on antibiotic resistance and recommendations for treatments.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIf successful, the team hopes the AI tool will help to combat antimicrobial resistance, a major global health concern, by preventing the mis-prescribing of antibiotics.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EOnce developed, the team will evaluate the effectiveness of the AI tool in helping to diagnose and treat diarrhoeal diseases in children compared with standard practices.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe tool will first be tested in a pilot study in eight healthcare facilities in Ethiopia and Uganda. Based on the results, the tool will be revised and improved before it is tested in a larger scale clinical trial across 40 facilities with up to 4,700 children.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"This project is truly going beyond what we do now. We’re going to move AI and digital technologies beyond the laboratory to understand how they can be harnessed in low- and middle-income countries to tackle real-world problems.","source":"Professor Tania Dottorini"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EIf successful, the new tool could help to speed up diagnoses, ensure children receive the treatments they need and relieve pressures on healthcare systems in places where resources are limited.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E&ldquo;Living in Africa as a child, I saw the impact these diseases could have. As a mother, to see your child suffering is terrible. I want to make a difference for children, mothers and families in places where there is limited access to doctors and healthcare, and I think this project offers us the chance to do that,&rdquo; added Professor Dottorini.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe project partners include King&rsquo;s College London, Makerere University in Uganda, the Addis Ababa City Administration Health Bureau in Ethiopia, Jembi Health Systems in South Africa, the University of Perugia in Italy and Causal Foundry Inc., in Spain.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003EReferences\u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Col\u003E\n\u003Cli\u003EUNICEF Data (2024). \u003Cem\u003EDiarrhoea.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E Available at: \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fdata.unicef.org\u002Ftopic\u002Fchild-health\u002Fdiarrhoeal-disease\u002F\"\u003Ehttps:\u002F\u002Fdata.unicef.org\u002Ftopic\u002Fchild-health\u002Fdiarrhoeal-disease\u002F\u003C\u002Fa\u003E (accessed 16 April by Heal Capital with participation from Angelini Ventures, IP Group and Daycrest, the round also includes follow-on investment from HEARN Partners, Redalphine, LifeX Ventures and ZEISS Ventures, as well as strategic investment from SAGES Ingenuity &ndash; the for-profit innovation arm of the Society of American Gastrointestinal and Endoscopic Surgeons (SAGES) and Macmillan Cancer Support, the UK&rsquo;s leading cancer charity.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EA King&rsquo;s College London spinout working as part of the London Institute for Healthcare Engineering (LIHE) and St Thomas&rsquo; MedTech ecosystem, Hypervision was founded by Dr Michael Ebner, a King&rsquo;s post-doctoral researcher at the time, Tom Vercauteren, Professor of Interventional Image Computing, Jonathan Shapey, Clinical Reader and Professor Sebastien Ourselin FREng FMedSci, Head of School of Biomedical Engineering &amp; Imaging Sciences.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe company will use the funding to accelerate commercial deployment and expand clinical adoption of its next generation hyperspectral sensing technologies.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"“This Series A marks a major milestone in our mission to power surgical intelligence through hyperspectral vision. By combining advanced spectral sensing with cloud-enabled AI analytics, we are building a new intelligence layer in surgery—giving surgeons real-time insights into tissue that were previously impossible to access.”","source":"Michael Ebner, CEO and Co-Founder, Hypervision Surgical"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EHyperspectral imaging uses light-matter interactions between wavelengths and biological materials to reveal what the naked eye cannot &ndash; including important tissue health characteristics such as composition and blood oxygenation.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe technology aims to break down visual limitations for surgeons, who despite advances in digital and robotic surgery still mostly rely on sight examination to guide important intraoperative decisions, potentially improving clinical decision-making and tissue health diagnosis during operations.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"“Surgical AI has made impressive progress using conventional cameras, but it ultimately faces a ceiling—it captures how tissue looks, not what is happening beneath the surface. Hypervision removes that limitation by delivering real-time, quantitative insights into tissue physiology at the pixel level. We believe this represents the sensing layer on which the next generation of surgical systems will be built—one that will make surgery measurably safer. That’s why we are proud to lead this round.”","source":"Christian Lautner, Founding Managing Partner, Heal Capital"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EHyperspectral Intelligence&reg; is Hypervision&rsquo;s platform technology. It combines proprietary on-chip spectral sensing, patented AI analytics, and a cloud-scalable architecture. Together, these technologies transform surgical imaging into real-time, data-driven decision support, generating previously invisible pixel-level insights into tissue physiology and composition during surgery.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"image","value":{"altText":"Hyperspectral Intelligence surgical technology","transformations":null,"caption":"Hyperspectral Intelligence®: Hypervision’s spectral chip and AI seamlessly integrate into existing vision systems, delivering quantitative tissue insights beyond conventional, subjective vision","asset":{"entryTitle":"HVS HI","entryDescription":"Hyperspectral Intelligence®: Hypervision’s spectral chip and AI seamlessly integrate into existing vision systems, delivering quantitative tissue insights beyond conventional, subjective vision. ","sys":{"id":"d83df3d1-c6d2-4bdf-b882-355405931834","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Ffolsm\u002Fhero-banner\u002Fbmeis\u002Fhvs-hi.x74889692.png"}}}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EHYPERSNAP&copy;, is the initial clinical deployment of Hyperspectral Intelligence&reg; and Hypervision&rsquo;s first commercial surgical system. It is designed to bring high-performance edge computing capabilities to power real-time reasoning in the operating theatre. The technology is certified in the UK and cleared by the US FDA for open and minimally-invasive general surgery.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"image","value":{"altText":"HYPERSNAP surgical technology","transformations":null,"caption":"HYPERSNAP®, Hypervision’s first commercial surgical system, shown here in an operating theatre environment, representing the initial clinical deployment of the Hyperspectral Intelligence® platform","asset":{"entryTitle":"HVS HS","entryDescription":"HYPERSNAP®, Hypervision’s first commercial surgical system, shown here in an operating theatre environment, representing the initial clinical deployment of the Hyperspectral Intelligence® platform","sys":{"id":"aa457a5e-67cd-4a22-8caa-b205cc46a8ab","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Ffolsm\u002Fhero-banner\u002Fbmeis\u002Fhvs-hs.x17657b5f.png"}}}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EHypervision&rsquo;s Series A funding will be used to build on the learnings gained from over 170 surgical procedures conducted across three UK NHS trusts so far. St Thomas&rsquo; Hospital has been a major contributor to the clinical evaluation of HYPERSNAP, with more than 60 colorectal procedures performed, including the very first. The funding will also be used to help expand use across more UK, European and US sites.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe funding will also advance development of the company&rsquo;s next-generation hyperspectral sensing technology &ndash; \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fwww.imec-int.com\u002Fen\u002Fpress\u002Fhypervision-advances-surgical-intelligence-next-generation-ai-enabled-spectral-imaging\"\u003Eco-developed with imec\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, a global leader in semiconductor and spectral sensing technologies &ndash; towards a scalable, cloud-enabled surgical intelligence platform.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe St Thomas&rsquo; MedTech ecosystem &ndash; including LIHE, the Surgical &amp; Interventional Engineering Research Centre and St Thomas&rsquo; Hospital &ndash; has been instrumental to Hypervision&rsquo;s commercial success with Ebner saying it helped to &ldquo;reduce barriers to complex surgical device development, significantly accelerating product development and clinical evaluation cycles&rdquo;.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"“Hypervision is an excellent example of how ecosystems, like the London Institute for Healthcare Engineering and St Thomas’ Medtech hub, work to accelerate the translation of innovative research into commercial successes with human benefits. I am excited to watch Hypervision build on the progress of its surgical innovations as it works towards widespread clinical adoption.” ","source":"Sir Bashir M. Al-Hashimi, Vice President (Research & Innovation), King’s College London"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EFind out more about Hypervision's Series A funding on \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Feur03.safelinks.protection.outlook.com\u002F?url=https%3A%2F%2Fnews.sky.com%2Fstory%2Fmark-kleinman-blog-see-the-latest-stories-from-sky-news-city-editor-13475361%3Fpostid%3D11602481%23liveblog-body&amp;data=05%7C02%7Cbmeis_comms%40kcl.ac.uk%7C5379d9853194491839ea08dea52b726b%7C8370cf1416f34c16b83c724071654356%7C0%7C0%7C639129803530591494%7CUnknown%7CTWFpbGZsb3d8eyJFbXB0eU1hcGkiOnRydWUsIlYiOiIwLjAuMDAwMCIsIlAiOiJXaW4zMiIsIkFOIjoiTWFpbCIsIldUIjoyfQ%3D%3D%7C0%7C%7C%7C&amp;sdata=Bb90RTWWcbyavi%2FU5kqirYbULPFt6ZxHnzrBoNQn%2FvI%3D&amp;reserved=0\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noopener\"\u003ESky News\u003C\u002Fa\u003E.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}},{"entryTitle":"Technology & Science","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"60c84afe-f51e-4e80-af7d-188d9eff995c","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"conversationNews":{"storyUrl":null},"doNot":false,"entryTitle":"King's College London spinout Hypervision Surgical raises £17m Series A funding to power surgical intelligence"},{"entryThumbnail":{"altText":"Carbon Neutral and ESG.Carbon Emission,Clean Energy.
