argue recycling and reuse can increase energy use, making emissions harder to cut.","relatedEvent":null,"title":"Why some green solutions can increase emissions","sys":{"owner":"k2584729","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fwhy-some-green-solutions-can-increase-emissions"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fwhy-some-green-solutions-can-increase-emissions","version":{"publishedBy":"k2031537","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2584729","created":"2026-04-27T08:11:49.442162Z","versionNo":"2.0","modified":"2026-04-28T07:14:43.9589336Z","modifiedBy":"k2031537","published":"2026-04-28T07:14:46.6980103Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"1f089596-23d6-43dd-8951-9f3ac0775ec8","projectId":"website","slug":"why-some-green-solutions-can-increase-emissions","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[],"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Jonatan Pinkse","entryDescription":"Research Director, Centre for Sustainable Business ","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"Jonatan Pinkse.jpg","caption":"","transformations":"w=240&h=160&crop=160,160,46,0","asset":{"entryTitle":"Jonatan Pinkse.jpg","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"3f754e45-dca7-4857-9283-9f67d47cf196","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002FImportedImages\u002FSchools\u002FBusiness\u002Fpeople\u002Facademics\u002Fjonatan-pinkse.jpg.xb521e082.jpg?w=240&h=160&crop=160,160,46,0"}}},"sys":{"id":"301ac28c-4719-4e75-a8d1-066f7deb7ea2","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fjonatan-pinkse"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003ENew research study from King&rsquo;s Business School argues that some circular economy strategies can increase emissions rather than reduce them.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EPublished in the British Journal of Management, the article presents a framework which shows that efforts to cut waste and switching to alternative materials can raise energy use. This can make it harder for firms to reduce greenhouse gas emissions at the same time. The framework was developed in collaboration with researchers from Imperial College London, the University of Greenwich, the University of Essex, the University of Bath and Oregon State University.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe insights come as businesses face growing pressure to meet environmental targets, a challenge likely to be in focus during Productivity Week 2026 (WC 27 April 2026).\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe research uses The LEGO Group to show how these tensions play out in practice.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe company explored alternative materials in 2023, including plant-based plastics, to reduce reliance on fossil fuels and support its sustainability goals. However, these materials proved difficult to scale while maintaining the durability and quality of LEGO bricks.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EAs a result, the changes did not automatically reduce overall emissions. In some cases, producing and processing alternative materials can require more energy, highlighting the trade-offs firms face when trying to cut both waste and emissions at the same time.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe study argues this is not unique to LEGO. Actions that support a circular economy, such as recycling or refurbishment, can increase energy demand across production systems.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe researchers describe a &ldquo;vicious cycle&rdquo;, where firms create new environmental trade-offs when pursuing different goals in isolation. They also identify a &ldquo;virtuous cycle&rdquo;, where firms redesign products and supply chains to better align net zero and circular economy aims.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"Different sustainability goals are often treated as if they naturally align. In practice, firms face real tensions. Managing those trade-offs is key to reducing environmental impact.","source":"Professor Jonatan Pinkse, Research Director at the Centre for Sustainable Business, King’s Business School"}},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"What matters is how firms manage these “vicious” and “virtuous” tensions. Companies need to rethink how products are designed and how supply chains work if they want sustainability goals to reinforce each other.","source":"Rajat Panwar, Professor of Responsible and Sustainable Business at Oregon State University"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe study suggests that reducing emissions requires more than individual green initiatives. Instead, firms need to coordinate changes across materials, processes and supply chains. The authors say policymakers may also need to recognise that different environmental targets can interact in unexpected ways.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003ERead the full study: \u003Ca href=\"https:\u002F\u002Fonlinelibrary.wiley.com\u002Fdoi\u002F10.1111\u002F1467-8551.70061\"\u003E\u003Cem\u003EReconciling circular economy and net zero: firm capabilities to resolve sustainability tensions\u003C\u002Fem\u003E\u003C\u002Fa\u003E, published in the British Journal of Management.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Business & Finance","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"d9e2ba7e-c2a5-46e8-8415-51d7602f62a8","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"conversationNews":{"storyUrl":null},"doNot":false,"entryTitle":"Why some green solutions can increase emissions"},{"entryThumbnail":{"altText":"Pregant people in a row","transformations":"w=834&h=440&crop=780,440,27,0","caption":"","asset":{"entryTitle":"Pregant people in a row. stigma","sys":{"owner":"k2480818","metadata":{},"workflow":{"id":"contensisEntryApproval","state":"versionComplete"},"dataFormat":"entry","isPublished":true,"availableLanguages":["en-GB"],"contentTypeId":"news","language":"en-GB","allUris":["\u002Fnews\u002Fmore-than-half-of-women-with-gestational-diabetes-subjected-to-dangerous-stigma"],"uri":"\u002Fnews\u002Fmore-than-half-of-women-with-gestational-diabetes-subjected-to-dangerous-stigma","version":{"publishedBy":"k2480818","archived":null,"deleted":null,"createdBy":"k2480818","created":"2026-04-22T09:43:26.7725668Z","versionNo":"1.0","modified":"2026-04-22T09:54:42.9322707Z","modifiedBy":"k2480818","published":"2026-04-22T09:54:45.9977826Z","archivedBy":null,"deletedBy":null},"versionStatus":"published","id":"f9060260-2888-4e72-9c6c-f9287bb530a5","projectId":"website","slug":"more-than-half-of-women-with-gestational-diabetes-subjected-to-dangerous-stigma","properties":{}},"relatedLinks":[],"intranet":false,"relatedCourses":[],"relatedPeople":[{"entryTitle":"Angus Forbes","entryDescription":"Professor of Diabetes Nursing","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"Image of Angus Forbes","transformations":"w=160&h=160","asset":{"entryTitle":"Angus Forbes","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"71ed5cda-853e-4be0-9c29-7a3b9462f74d","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fnmpc\u002Fpeople\u002Fangus-forbes.xa22aa274.png?w=160&h=160"}}},"sys":{"id":"bd9e54e2-24bb-40a1-a452-2b4136985833","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fangus-forbes"}},{"entryTitle":"Judith Parsons","entryDescription":"Lecturer and Researcher in Women's Health in Long-Term Conditions","entryThumbnail":{"altText":"","caption":"","transformations":"w=160&h=212&crop=160,160,0,14","asset":{"entryTitle":"Profile picture_Judith Parsons","entryDescription":null,"sys":{"id":"5fcda550-47a7-447c-b7f2-6bf187c4a3f1","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"image","dataFormat":"asset","uri":"\u002Fnewimages\u002Fperson-profile\u002F2022b\u002Fprofile-picture-judith-parsons.x4e761c97.jpg?w=160&h=212&crop=160,160,0,14"}}},"sys":{"id":"8c88919d-0220-4512-ae2e-ed6198e79045","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"person","dataFormat":"entry","uri":"\u002Fpeople\u002Fjudith-parsons"}}],"authorByline":null,"articleContent":[{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EThe new research led by King&rsquo;s College London and University College Cork and funded by Diabetes UK has revealed the scale of stigma experienced by women with gestational diabetes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003E\u003Cstrong\u003E \u003C\u002Fstrong\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EResearchers surveyed 1,800 women across the UK who had experienced GDM and conducted focus groups to understand the drivers of stigma and develop tools to combat its harmful effects.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EResearchers surveyed 1,800 women across the UK who had experienced GDM and conducted focus groups to understand the drivers of stigma and develop tools to combat its harmful effects.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EDiabetes stigma comes in many forms - from throwaway comments to false assumptions. Its impacts are severe - stigma can stop some people living with diabetes from seeking the support they need and evidence shows this can lead to higher blood sugar levels and increases their risk of diabetes complications.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe new research funded by Diabetes UK and led by researchers at Kings College London and University College Cork surveyed 1,800 women across the UK who had experienced GDM and conducted focus groups to understand the drivers of stigma and develop tools to combat its harmful effects.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe survey revealed that many women experienced emotional distress at diagnosis, with over two thirds (68%) reporting anxiety, 58% feeling upset and 48% experiencing fear. For many, the psychological impact continued beyond birth, with 61% saying GDM negatively affected their feelings about future pregnancies.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EExperiences of stigma and misunderstanding were widespread. Nearly half (49%) of women felt judged for having GDM, while 47% felt judged because of their body size. Over 80% felt that other people did not understand GDM, and more than a third (36%) concealed their diagnosis from others.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EGDM stigma was also frequently experienced in healthcare settings, with 48% saying professionals made assumptions about their diet and exercise, and more than half (52%) feeling judged based on their blood glucose results.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EMany women described a loss of control and a sense of disruption to their pregnancy experience. Nearly two thirds (64%) felt they were denied a &lsquo;normal&rsquo; pregnancy, while 76% reported a lack of control over their pregnancy. More than a third (36%) of women felt abandoned by healthcare services after giving birth, and one in four (25%) continued to experience depression or anxiety postpartum.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EIn additional focus groups, respondents described encountering harmful stereotypes from loved ones or even professionals in healthcare settings including assumptions that they were &lsquo;lazy&rsquo;, had &lsquo;poor eating habits&rsquo; or &lsquo;lacked willpower&rsquo;. Respondents commonly experienced stigmatising phrases from family and friends relating to eating and weight such as &ldquo;\u003Cem\u003Eshould you be eating that?\u003C\u002Fem\u003E&rdquo; and \u003Cem\u003E&ldquo;you must have eaten too much, that&rsquo;s why you have GDM.\u003C\u002Fem\u003E&rdquo;\u003C\u002Fp\u003E\n\u003Cp\u003EThe researchers are calling for targeted interventions to reduce stigma, alongside structured emotional support for women during and after pregnancies affected by GDM, to improve both mental and physical health outcomes.\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"},{"type":"quote","value":{"text":"Stigma and emotional distress are far more common in women diagnosed with gestational diabetes (GDM) than many realise. Everyday interactions - even with those who mean well - can deepen this harm, shaping women’s emotional wellbeing and the choices they feel able to make. It’s clear that meaningful action is needed to protect women’s mental and physical health.”","source":"Professor Angus Forbes, Professor of Diabetes Nursing at King’s College London"}},{"type":"markup","value":"\u003Cp\u003EElizabeth Robertson, Director of Research and Clinical at Diabetes UK, said: &ldquo;Stigma can have a dangerous and devastating impact on pregnant women diagnosed with gestational diabetes, particularly at a time when emotions and anxieties may already be heightened. We know that stigma can lead to shame, isolation and poorer mental health, and may discourage people from attending healthcare appointments, potentially increasing the risk of serious complications. This research highlights the urgent need for better support systems, based on understanding and empathy to ensure no one feels blamed or judged during their pregnancy.&rdquo;\u003C!-- [if !supportLineBreakNewLine]--\u003E\u003C!--[endif]--\u003E\u003C\u002Fp\u003E"}],"categories":[{"entryTitle":"Health","entryDescription":null,"entryThumbnail":null,"sys":{"id":"505c6ef3-27ad-49ad-ab3b-31289188963a","language":"en-GB","contentTypeId":"categories","dataFormat":"entry","uri":null}}],"conversationNews":{"storyUrl":null},"doNot":false,"entryTitle":"More than half of women with gestational diabetes subjected to 'dangerous' stigma"}],"thumbnailImage":{"altText":"Strand Campus, King's College London.
